#### Abstracts
##### **OpenVLHarness: Real World Visual Agentic Tool-Use with Multimodal Memory**
2610.26860v1 by Yu Zhou, Cheng-Fu Yang, Rui Sun, Di Wu, Sibo Peng, Mingyang Zhang, Zhen Yang, Ruiyi Zhang, Nanyun Peng, Zhe Gan, Kai-Wei Chang

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress on visual reasoning, yet many tasks require evidence that is difficult to obtain through internal reasoning alone: our controlled study on 22 benchmarks shows that simply increasing the inference budget of the base model yields only modest gains. Such tasks demand grounded perception, external knowledge, and numerical measurements obtained through heterogeneous tools, whose outputs must be made interpretable to the model and retained across reasoning steps. We introduce OpenVLHarness, a multimodal reasoning harness organized around a capability layer and persistent multimodal memory. The capability layer combines task-level tool interfaces with evidence rendering, providing consistent access to heterogeneous backends and converting their outputs into model-readable observations. Persistent multimodal memory retains the corresponding images, structured data, and web references as addressable artifacts throughout each query. A coding interface executes generated programs over stored data, enabling computations that combine results from multiple tools. Together, these mechanisms support an iterative process of acquiring, inspecting, and composing multimodal evidence. Evaluated on 22 benchmarks with eight open and proprietary backbones, OpenVLHarness improves average scores by 9.7-12.6 points over matched no-tool baselines, including +11.2 with Qwen3-VL-8B, +9.7 with Kimi K3, and +9.9 with GPT-6 Astra. With GPT-6 Luna at medium reasoning effort, OpenVLHarness outperforms the base model, Codex, and Visual Sketchpad at maximum effort by 7.7-8.5 points while incurring at most 37% of their model-token cost.

摘要：

##### **Can It Actually Work? Synthesizing Functional CAD Mechanisms**
2610.26861v1 by Wenqian Zhang, Yangyi Huang, Zigeng Xu, Weiyang Liu, Zhen Liu

While LLM agents can generate visually plausible CAD assemblies, these designs often fail to fulfill their intended physical functions. We study functional CAD design, a setting where agents interpret underspecified requests to construct and iteratively revise mechanisms that satisfy functional requirements in physical simulation. Verifying these requirements is nontrivial: different candidate mechanisms may realize the same function through entirely different components, controls, and operating sequences. To bridge this gap, we propose FunCAD-Harness, a framework that translates abstract requirements into candidate-specific physical tests and uses simulation feedback to guide design revisions. To systematically evaluate this capability, we introduce FunCADBench, a benchmark spanning 46 mechanism families with interdependent and multi-step operations. Experiments across frontier LLM agents show that FunCAD-Harness exposes subtle mechanical failures invisible to visual inspection, substantially improving functional success rates over standard simulation-augmented baselines. Ultimately, our work marks a step toward CAD agents capable of translating human intent into working mechanisms.

摘要：

##### **WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions**
2610.26847v1 by Rui Shi, Xunjian Yin, Jiacheng Sang, Zichuan Liu, Shuyan Zhou

Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.

摘要：

##### **Train An Imaginative Agent**
2610.26845v1 by Jiaxin Ge, Junyi Zhang, Tianning Chai, Hanjun Yoo, Chenyang Wang, Xiuyu Li, Weijia Shi, XuDong Wang, Sewon Min, Luke Zettlemoyer, Trevor Darrell, Haiwen Feng

A seemingly reasonable push in Sokoban can make the puzzle unsolvable. To avoid such mistakes, an agent has to anticipate the consequences of its actions before execution, especially when feedback from the real environment is sparse or actions are irreversible. We introduce Agentic Imagination (Agima), a training framework that trains an agent to imagine before acting, i.e. to be imaginative. Agima trains a single omni model to propose actions, generate images of their outcomes, and then reason over these imagined futures to decide what to execute in the real environment. Rather than committing to a single plan, the agent can simulate a sequence of actions, explore alternatives, and compare possible futures. We first use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to unify policy and world modeling within a single omni model. This enables the agent to predict future outcomes and perform basic forms of imagination, such as forward thinking and counterfactual branching. We then use agentic reinforcement learning (RL) to teach the agent to reason with its imagined futures and use them effectively for planning. Across four environments spanning Maze2D, Sokoban, Pacman, and PushT, Agima generalizes to harder settings unseen during training. Trained with SFT alone on two-box Sokoban, Agima solves 40% of four-box puzzles while seeing the real board only once every five moves, compared with an action-only planner that only solves 2%. After RL, new behaviors emerge in how Agima uses its imagination: it learns a new unified format that was unseen during the SFT stage, learns to conduct forward thinking only when necessary, and branches more frequently at critical decision points. An agent that can picture where its actions lead can learn when and where to look ahead, that is, to plan. Project page: https://agima.net/.

摘要：

##### **AnyGoal: On World Model Generalization Beyond Expert Rollouts**
2610.26844v1 by Heejeong Nam, Divyat Mahajan, Quentin Le Lidec, Randall Balestriero, David Klindt

The goal of a world model is to capture the dynamics of an environment. This has the great appeal that it allows agents to plan and simulate a course of actions with the help of a world model. For these uses to be reliable, however, the learned dynamics must behave well beyond the specific state-action combinations observed in expert rollouts. Despite this, the relationship between data coverage, dynamics generalization, and downstream planning performance remains underexplored. We study this relationship through the lens of compositional generalization: can a world model recombine familiar state and action factors into valid dynamics? To answer this, we introduce AnyGoal, a benchmark of goal-shifted task variants built from widely used world-model environments, together with an open-source library. Each task is designed to be within reach of a world model that has learned the underlying dynamics: individual state factors and actions remain within their training support, but their combinations are underrepresented in expert trajectories. Across multiple environments and world model families, we find that current models consistently exhibit failures under these recombinations. By examining dynamics and representation failures across model families, interaction regimes, and evaluation metrics, we study where current world models generalize and where challenges remain for goal-shifted prediction and planning. Together, this framework, benchmark, and analysis provide a basis for evaluating progress toward world models that generalize to new planning tasks.

摘要：

##### **LatComm: Reusable Context Compression for Latent Communication between Large Language Models**
2610.26840v1 by Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tan Yu, Weijie Zhao

Systems that combine several large language models (LLMs) often use one context for many questions. When the context is passed between models as text, the receiving model processes all of its tokens again for every question. Avoiding this cost requires a context representation that is compact, reusable across questions, and readable by a different LLM. Existing compression and latent communication methods each provide only some of these properties. We introduce LatComm, a Latent Communication interface that provides all three by separating encoding from reading: the context is encoded once without the question, and the receiver selects what to read for each question. A frozen context encoder LLM compresses the context into a fixed-size packet and sends it to a different frozen receiver LLM, which reads the packet through cross-attention, with gates computed from its prompt. On 12 multiple-choice question-answering datasets, LatComm improves average accuracy over single-vector baselines by up to 10.2 points. On QuALITY, it reduces the receiver's input by 57x and answers the questions of an article 3x faster.

摘要：

##### **Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy**
2610.26836v1 by Ke Zhang, Sahchit Chundur, Jerry Peprah Owusu, Mohammad Javad Abdolhosseini Qomi, Maziar Raissi

LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.

摘要：

##### **Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention**
2610.26834v1 by Anamaria-Roberta Hartl, Levente Zólyomi, Michael List, Günter Klambauer, Sebastian Böck, Sepp Hochreiter

We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

摘要：

##### **Competing Learning in Hybrid Architectures**
2610.26830v1 by Xingyu Shen, Yingfa Chen, Ziqing Qiao, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han, Zhiyuan Liu

Many modern language models have adopted hybrid architectures that incorporate both linear-time recurrence and softmax attention. Modern recurrence layers provide a highly expressive mixing mechanism while ensuring high throughput by compressing contextual information into a fixed-size state, but underperform softmax attention in contextual information retrieval. In this paper, we reveal a Competing Learning phenomenon where different kinds of mixers compete to learn the same function, and when any layer learns a function, it suppresses the corresponding learning signal for other layers. Layer attribution analysis reveals that under typical data distributions, modern recurrence learns retrieval functions faster than softmax attention, causing attention to fail to learn retrieval in most cases. Stopping weight updates in recurrence in the middle of training helps attention learn retrieval. Based on these results, we propose a staged training procedure for pre-training hybrid architectures on natural language. Empirical results on models up to 1B parameters show that our method results in substantial improvement in language modeling loss, common-sense reasoning, as well as a wide range of retrieval-related tasks across different context lengths.

摘要：

##### **ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry**
2610.26829v1 by Matt Turk, Brandon L. Neel, Diana M. Lopez, Zachary D. Smith, Grace N. Johnecheck, James B. Golden

Frontier agents retrieve structural metadata well and compute geometry from coordinates less reliably. We ask how much of a benchmark score reflects that gap and how much reflects the evaluation configuration around the model. We study ProteinGeoBench, a suite of 131 protein-structure tasks, using 4,249 rollouts from four frontier models and 4,486 further trials across 26 models. Three of the four frontier models fall within 0.031 of one another (0.785 to 0.816) with no difference between them resolved, while the fourth scores 0.707 and all three gaps to it exclude zero. Three of the four do better on metadata lookup than on numeric calculation. The fourth is slightly better on numeric. Numeric tasks are 28 of the 131 but half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail. The ranking also depends on the evaluation budget: with one run per task, 23.9% of bootstrap replicates put a different model first. On a selected 15-task slice, removing network access lowers scores more than blocking eight structural-database domains, but the two contrasts were graded in different passes and cannot be cleanly decomposed. Swapping in each model's own command-line agent changes scores by -0.071 to +0.086, a comparison too small to rule out moderate harness effects. Across 26 models, numeric performance rises with non-numeric performance rather than staying flat. The experiments use different task subsets, so they cannot rank model, harness, access and task composition against one another. Evaluations should report results by task family, along with the number of runs, the harness and the access conditions.

##### **Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis**
2610.26828v1 by Zhaoyang Wang, Qianhui Wu, Haorui Wang, Simon Yu, Baolin Peng, Canwen Xu, Hao Cheng, Wenlin Yao, Boyi Liu, Yite Wang, Jianfeng Gao, Huaxiu Yao

Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM.

摘要：

##### **KEEP-Bench: Evaluating Evidence Preservation in Context Compression**
2610.26827v1 by Ziqing Man, Di Wu, Jia-Chen Gu, Junyi Zhang, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng

Context compression improves long-context efficiency but may introduce faithfulness issues through an information bottleneck. Existing evaluations rely on QA metrics, but a reader can still answer correctly from an unfaithful compression, so these metrics underestimate such loss. To bridge this gap, we introduce KEEP-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that directly assesses compression faithfulness with 4,102 cases across three challenging settings that require keeping evidence for plausible reference resolutions, explicit qualifiers, and minority perspectives. Experiments show that even with tailored prompting, the strongest models remain unreliable: despite context windows exceeding 100K tokens and inputs averaging only 3.1K tokens, the best open-weight model (31B) preserves all required evidence in only 66.8% of cases, and the best proprietary model in only 61.6%. Performance also varies sharply across evidence structures, so conclusions from limited structures may be biased. We further introduce a metric, Evidence Preservation Return (EPR), to relate evidence recall to inference FLOPs, and find that it peaks at moderate scales (4B-8B), where recall remains low. Additional output budget mainly benefits already strong models and widens capability gaps, yet the stronger preservation of these models comes at a steep computational cost. These findings highlight the importance for future work to investigate compressors with strong information preservation under a small computation budget, rather than relying on a large output budget or model scaling.

##### **RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development**
2610.26826v1 by Yuchi Wang, Rongyao Fang, Weihao Yu, Xilin Wei, Tiancheng Gu, Dingkang Yang, Hang Zhang, Dayiheng Liu, Hongsheng Li, Shuai Bai

Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.

##### **MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution**
2610.26825v1 by Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jingsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong

Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

##### **AfriBED: a Speech Recognition Bias Evaluation Dataset for Low Resource African Languages**
2610.26821v1 by Claytone Sikasote, Hussein Suleman, Jan Buys

Despite advancements in automatic speech recognition systems (ASR), current systems exhibit speaker-attribute bias, i.e., their speech recognition performance is biased against certain speakers based on their demographic attributes such as gender, accents, and speaker type. However, accurately assessing speaker attribute bias in a given ASR system requires the availability of a bias evaluation dataset with comparable speech across subgroups of a speaker attribute. In this paper, we introduce African speech Bias Evaluation Dataset (AfriBED), a novel speech recognition bias evaluation dataset for two low resource African languages, Bemba and Nyanja, to detect and estimate gender and speaker-type bias for ASR systems. The dataset comprises 9 hours of speech with 3928 utterances recorded by 80 speakers for Bemba, while the Nyanja set includes 6260 utterances with approximately 18 hours of speech recorded by 127 speakers. The results of our baseline experiments demonstrate that the datasets are useful for examining gender and speaker-type attribute bias in the target languages.

摘要：

##### **ASTOR: Benchmarking and Improving Autoformulation for Soft-Constraint Optimization with a Formal Grammar**
2610.26819v1 by Amer Goel, Yeganeh Alimohammadi, Andrew Daw

Real-world decision problems combine two qualitatively different kinds of constraints: requirements that must be satisfied, called hard constraints, and preferences that can be violated, called soft constraints. Because these problems are often posed to decision-makers in natural language, large language models offer a natural tool for assisting with their solution. This has given rise to autoformulation, which focuses on using LLMs to translate natural-language descriptions of decision problems into executable mathematical optimization models. However, this research often neglects soft constraints like those proposed by consumers, managers, and planners, despite the fact that many practical problems require modeling them explicitly. We introduce ASTOR, an inference-time framework that maps a natural-language decision problem to a grammar-guided abstract syntax tree (AST) before deterministically compiling the resulting representation into executable optimization code. We also introduce SoftOR, a unified benchmark of natural language problems which focuses on soft constraint formulations. On SoftOR, ASTOR achieves the best aggregate accuracy among comparable single-request and multi-agent LLM methods while remaining competitive on standard autoformulation benchmarks that include only hard constraints. Code and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/astor-softor-iclr-E34C/README.md.

摘要：

##### **Next Best Action: Causal Constrained Offline RL for Business Action Targeting**
2610.26817v1 by Surya Ramachandiran, Yiming Li, Juan Mancilla-Caceres, Tao Ye, Jun Liu

Next Best Action (NBA) systems must select among customer interventions (e.g., discounts, promotions, and lifecycle actions), maximize long-term economic value, satisfy complex operational constraints, and remain causally grounded. Existing approaches lack a rigorous framework for meeting these requirements jointly. We address this gap by formulating NBA as a Constrained Markov Decision Process (CMDP). We then integrate off-policy causal correction of the estimated customer dynamics directly into the Linear Programming (LP) formulation of the CMDP. The resulting policy selects actions based on customer states to maximize long-term value while explicitly enforcing business guardrails. Furthermore, the LP formulation yields shadow prices that quantify the economic trade-offs of operational constraints (e.g., fairness and user welfare), enhancing policy interpretability. We validate our proposed offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework on an industry case study involving rideshare coupon targeting, where customer states are defined by Recency, Frequency, and Monetary (RFM) stages. We evaluate performance using model-based off-policy evaluation, with causal dynamics estimated from a temporal holdout. Under the observed coupon budget and RFM state representation, our optimized policy improves customer lifetime value (LTV) by 19% over the estimated behavior policy and 8% over a myopic baseline. Although the demonstrated action space is binary, the proposed framework is action-agnostic, yielding a general-purpose decision module for targeting systems that manage multiple interventions.

##### **Safety Mid-Training: Internalizing LLM Safety as a Foundational Capability**
2610.26809v1 by Zhengyue Zhao, Liwei Jiang, Yejin Choi, Chaowei Xiao1

Safety alignment for large language models (LLMs) is primarily performed during post-training, while recent work has also begun incorporating safety data into pre-training. Yet safety can degrade when later training no longer includes safety data, and gains in safety often come at the cost of general capability. This tension suggests that safety and general reasoning should not be treated as separate training goals. We instead view safety as an LLM capability that can be learned through a reasoning structure shared with general tasks. Both require the model to understand intent or task goals, apply relevant knowledge, and reason about potential consequences. We introduce safety mid-training, a stage between pre-training and post-training that jointly develops safety and general reasoning through this shared structure. Given safety and utility prompts, the base model generates structured documents and reasoning traces that are then used for mid-training. Safety mid-training reduces average attack success rate (ASR) by 72% to 81% relative to Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B base models, while also improving general capability. The benefits persist through subsequent post-training. After general-only SFT, mid-trained models largely retain their safety, achieving lower ASR than the baselines without sacrificing general capability. They also retain safety under general-only GRPO without safety rewards, while subsequent RL reaches a better safety-utility frontier when initialized from the mid-trained models. Together, these results suggest that learning safety and general reasoning within a shared mid-training stage can reduce the safety-utility trade-off and make safety more robust to subsequent training.

摘要：

##### **Teacher Supervision, Fixed Representations and Calibration: Exact Risk Calculations for Candidate-Scoring Decision Models**
2610.26797v1 by Agus Sudjianto, Denis Burakov

Candidate-scoring decision models return a probability distribution over a presented set of answers. Public documentation of one such system, Jev, and of an open counterpart, JevAny, describes three training ingredients that determine what these probabilities estimate: soft targets from larger models, a frozen pretrained backbone and temperature calibration. We analyze each ingredient in Gaussian surrogates where the finite-sample risk can be computed exactly. A student trained on teacher targets inherits the teacher's bias, which more student data does not remove and agreement with the teacher does not reveal. A fixed representation lowers the risk only below an explicit sample-size threshold. In a probit model the exact recalibration map is a temperature scaling, which removes all excess risk only when the teacher's coefficient has the correct direction and otherwise leaves a residual that calibration diagnostics do not detect. Under Luce's choice axiom, candidate scores are coherent across refinements of an event if and only if they aggregate by log-sum-exp. On 231,521 credit card complaints from the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, where the decision is whether a complaint ends in monetary relief, a student trained on a logistic teacher's probabilities ends at the teacher's decision cost while a student trained on outcomes goes 8% below it, a 30B model read zero-shot remains worse than the teacher after recalibration, and that model departs from the coherence condition by 17.5 decibans. Pretrained representations reduce the labeled data required and calibration corrects the scale of the probabilities, but neither corrects a biased teacher, so the probabilities have to be checked against realized outcomes.

摘要：

##### **SOMA: Continuous Monitoring and Intervention for System Two Reasoning with System One Models**
2610.26793v1 by Ruizhi Qian, Lei Shi, Tianyu Hu, Bo Yan, Song Wang, Yu Tian

Reasoning models often run into problems without noticing them, even when symptoms are visible in their own text. In humans, metacognition separates monitoring from control: fast, intuitive signals keep watch while slow, deliberate reasoning unfolds and help decide whether to continue, change course, or stop. System One (S1) decision models, such as Jev, are a natural candidate for this role: they return a calibrated probability for a natural-language question about a text in one fast call, yet they are used only as gates before reasoning. We introduce SOMA (System One Metacognitive Awareness), in which an S1 model continuously monitors a System Two (S2) reasoning model and intervenes when needed. SOMA has three layers: a scenario layer states each objective in natural language with predefined actions, an awareness layer lets S1 judge whether the described behavior appears, and a control layer accumulates these judgments, first reminds S2, and takes over only if the behavior persists. Sharing one S1 model and one controller across tasks, without training, SOMA addresses two different failures on two reasoning models: on overthinking, it cuts reasoning tokens by up to 36\% while improving average accuracy, and on unanswerable questions, it raises abstention from 70.5\% to 87.2\%.

##### **LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences**
2610.26787v1 by Lu Sun, Xiangyi Zhang, Xiangyang Zhu, Kaiyuan Ji, Zijian Chen, Qiang Hu, Zicheng Zhang, Yuan Tian, Guangtao Zhai

Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.

##### **RESIN: Multi-Chain Reasoning with Entity-Structured Indexing**
2610.26782v1 by Xuanyu Meng, Jash Rajesh Parekh, Jiawei Han

Multi-chain reasoning is a challenging task not only because the multi-chain evidence is dispersed, but also because different parts of reasoning may require distinct, interacting chains of support. Flat retrieval can flood a model with loosely related text, whereas early selection of a single reasoning path can discard evidence that becomes useful only after other facts are considered. We introduce RESIN (Reasoning with Entity Structured Indexing), which uses structure to locate evidence while keeping the original source as the basis for reasoning. RESIN decomposes a question into focused one-hop needs, retrieves a small set of source-grounded units for each hop, and places only this relevant evidence in a compact reasoning buffer. The buffer preserves alternative supports until final composition, making intermediate decisions explicit without forcing a brittle path. On the full HolisQA benchmark, RESIN achieves 82.92 context recall and 76.70 answer relevancy over all questions, with higher reported CR and AR across five domains and higher F1 in four domains than the published baselines. Ablations show that both typed evidence diversity and hop-wise decomposition are essential, supporting a clear conclusion: Precise evidence organization and delayed commitment make multi-chain reasoning effective and auditable. Our code is available at https://github.com/RamonMeng/RESIN.

##### **Game of Phones: Harnessing Game Theory and LLMs in 'Bot Wars' to Counteract Phone Scams**
2610.26781v1 by Nardine Basta, Robin Carpentier, Benjamin Zi Hao Zhao, Dali Kaafar

Phone scams impose substantial and growing costs on individuals and financial systems worldwide, yet existing countermeasures impose no resource cost on perpetrators. Strategic scam-baiting, in which automated agents engage scammers to deplete their operational capacity, offers a proactive alternative but requires dynamic strategy adaptation that static systems cannot provide. We introduce a game-theoretic framework enabling LLMs to learn effective counter-strategies through in-context experience without fine-tuning. The framework models scammer-baiter interactions as a two-player game with empirically grounded strategies and utility functions, using Nash equilibrium as a formal benchmark for evaluating learned behaviour. Across 300 simulated dialogues and three LLM architectures, adaptive strategy selection achieves 28.9-79.7% closer alignment to Nash equilibrium than non-adaptive baselines. The code and an annotated dataset of 300 dialogues with turn-level strategy labels and utility scores are released at https://github.com/nardinebasta/GameofPhones_/ .

##### **Monsoon: Scalable Spontaneous Speech Collection for Low-Resource ASR in 50 Languages**
2610.26776v1 by Shobhit Banga, Manas Dhir, Aaditya Pareek, Amritansh Walecha, Bhaskar Singh, Hanuman Sidh, Manmeet Kaur, Sagar Jain, Supriya Paul, Vanshika Chhabra, Utkarsh Singh

For most languages, automatic speech recognition (ASR) is limited less by model architecture than by a lack of accurately transcribed spontaneous speech. We present Monsoon, a framework for collecting such data at scale. It elicits unscripted conversations between paired contributors, screens recordings with automated classifiers, corrects transcripts over five levels of human review, and selects segments for lexical and acoustic coverage. It has collected speech in 50 languages; we analyse seven 3,500-hour corpora. At equal volume, its quality stages reduce semantic word error rate (WER) by 10-39% relative to a conventional protocol. Compared with an architecture-matched baseline trained on public corpora, Whisper fine-tuned on Monsoon performs comparably on the baseline's own test sets and reduces semantic WER by 20-44 points on conversational benchmarks. The gains transfer to a second architecture and grow with data volume. We release the framework, its quality-control models, and the fine-tuned models.

##### **HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs**
2610.26770v1 by Zhuoshi Pan, ZhiYuan Feng, Yukuo Cen, Honglin Lin, Junru Lu, Zhongxia Chen, Shuo Yang, H. Vicky Zhao, Jie Tang

Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. Full adaptation reaches 28.2% and 24.6%, respectively, while parameter-efficient adaptation substantially reduces unmatched outputs. On open-ended conditional generation, full MLM adaptation reaches BLEU/ROUGE-L of 0.163/0.583 on QQP and 0.107/0.327 on Quasar-T, placing it in the same broad performance range as published BERT models trained through diffusion without explicit diffusion training. Finally, applying the same generative interface to stronger masked representations substantially improves performance: RoBERTa-large reaches 44.2% on ARC-Easy and 33.8% on MMLU. These results separate three components of masked iterative generation: pretraining supplies the representation and denoising operator, task adaptation determines what the model generates, and an iterative commitment rule supplies the decoding process. Explicit diffusion training is therefore not necessary to obtain effective iterative conditional generation from pretrained MLMs in the settings studied here.

##### **Concerns in Benchmarking Jailbreaks: An Empirical Study of Attack Success Rates**
2610.26595v1 by Leon Tu Nguyen, Rohan Menon, Rafael Tappe Maestro, Eric Aubinais, Chih-Hong Cheng

Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.

##### **Beyond Attack Success Rate: Cost-Aware Metric for Evaluating Iterative LLM Jailbreaks**
2610.26589v1 by Rohan Menon, Chih-Hong Cheng

Attack success rate (ASR) evaluates jailbreaks against large language models without accounting for repeated calls to the victim model. Yet each additional call gives the deployed system another opportunity to detect the attack and intervene. We introduce $\mathrm{ASR}_{\mathrm{STEP}}$, which penalizes additional victim-model calls while retaining binary success judgments. Our evaluation considers GCG~\cite{zou2023universal}, PAIR~\cite{chao2025jailbreaking} with a step budget, and a single-step PAP~\cite{zeng2024johnny} baseline on JailBreakBench across three model families. GCG's scores decrease by 7.0--8.7 percentage points after discounting, while PAP's remain unchanged. While for PAIR~\cite{chao2025jailbreaking}, we see a wider discounting range between 3.4-12 percentage points, showing that the "step" quantity differs per iterative strategy. Reporting both metrics makes the discount due to repeated victim-model interaction explicit. Code is available \hyperlink{https://github.com/SuELS-Group/beyond_asr}{here}.

##### **WhatIf-Bench: Benchmarking Intervention-Aware Time Series Forecasting**

This enables scenario analysis: forecasting how a target responds to events that set covariates to chosen future values. Whether they can do this reliably is an open question. We introduce WhatIf-Bench, a benchmark measuring how well forecasters use covariates to forecast a target's response to future events. WhatIf-Bench is semi-synthetic: time series come from mechanistic simulators of data centers, power grids, road traffic, and water networks, with controlled covariate-target relationships grounded in each system. Each task presents a history of events intervening on covariates, then asks to forecast a future event that either replays seen values (Replay) or sets them to unseen ones (Novel). Varying the number of covariates, historical events, and event length yields 37,665 tasks. Evaluating TSFMs, frontier LLMs, and simple regression baselines, we find the LLMs reach the highest skill (improvement over a naive baseline), ahead of the best TSFMs. Every model forecasts well on Replay futures (48 to 85% skill) but drops to -7 to 50% on Novel futures, with the regression baselines falling the most. Skill on Novel futures also falls as events set more covariates jointly. More historical events raise skill on Novel futures across the board, but large differences remain. WhatIf-Bench reveals generalization to Novel futures as the core challenge, and offers a testbed toward arbitrary scenario analysis.

##### **Reward Objectives for Robust (PO)MDPs: A Law-Preserving Equivalence with (Partially Observable) Games**
2610.26568v1 by Durgam Latha, Dion Reji, Dorde Zikelic, Shankaranarayanan Krishna

We consider the problem of solving RPOMDPs under all four reward objectives, where the goal is to decide whether the value of a risk measure with respect to a reward objective exceeds a given threshold. We consider different risk measures, such as (1)~expected value, (2)~mean-variance (MV), (3)~conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), and (4)~entropic risk (ERisk). Our results can be summarized as follows. First, we formalize the class of law-invariant risk measures, that subsume all four risk measures above. Second, we propose a unified framework for solving RPOMDPs with discounted-sum, long-run average and cumulative reward objectives under any law-invariant risk measure, by reducing the problem to solving partially observable stochastic games (POSGs). Our unified framework applies to (s,a)-rectangular RPOMDPs with polytopic uncertainty sets, which we show to be {\em equivalent} to POSGs, for each of the four reward objectives and any law-invariant risk measure. We prove equivalence by designing logspace reductions between the RPOMDP and POSG models, that map strategies to strategies and preserve the distribution of the reward objective, up to a constant rescaling factor. Third, as a corollary, we derive new computational complexity results for R(PO)MDPs and POSGs. ##### **Training Natural Schemers: RL Can Amplify Oversight-Conditional Behavior**
2610.26551v1 by Bart Jaworski, Nathaniel Mitrani, Fred Bruford, Victoria Krakovna

One of the central concerns for the safety of advanced AI is scheming: when a model strategically complies under oversight but pursues its own goals when unobserved. Current demonstrations, such as Anthropic's Alignment Faking work, depend on design choices that make them less convincing: explicit test-time prompting, handcrafted backdoors, or training data that demonstrates or hints at the target behavior. Model organisms built in this way cannot tell us whether scheming tendencies would arise or increase under realistic training conditions. We therefore construct a natural model organism of scheming, built on Kimi K2.6: trained with traits that could plausibly emerge in a frontier training pipeline, and without providing the model with the target scheming policy. We then train it using Reinforcement Learning (RL) with a reward that scores only task quality and policy compliance and does not reward scheming; we show that this amplifies scheming. We measure the oversight flip (the gap between monitored and unmonitored compliance with a given policy) and find that it increases during RL training across our environments. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) analysis finds explicit instrumental reasoning about oversight accompanying this behavior. The effect holds across our three RL environments, and we find that the scheming policy transfers between them. The pipeline also replicates on other open-weight base models. Our results suggest that a reward that does not reward scheming offers no inherent protection against certain traits when an oversight-conditional policy is indistinguishable from an aligned one during training.

##### **Breaking Diffusion: Parallel Reasoning with Draft Refinement**
2610.26542v1 by Jaesang Yu, Seoyeon Byun, Yun Wang, Yan Lu, Juho Kim

Drafting an answer allows a reasoning model to reconsider each decision in the context of the others. Using the known corruption process, we correct for this self-token evidence to estimate how well each token is supported by the prompt and the remaining draft. ##### **A Fisher-Geometric View of Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation**
2610.26525v1 by Liu Kaiyuan, Zhuang Ziyuan, Ma Haoxuan, Gu Yannian, Song Mingfei, Weng Rongxiang, Ye Jieping

Extending tangent-space analyses of merging, we show that the same coefficients that mix task vectors also mix implicit rewards, which lets a residual program on unlabelled prompts solve for them. Phase~2 lets this spectrum govern what may be added: RGBM passivates domains with low normalized residuals, trains isolated branches only for domains above the spectral gap, and writes back the admitted updates with calibrated shrinkage. ##### **TeleASR-Amharic: A Benchmark for Robust Amharic ASR in Realistic Telecommunication Conditions**
2610.26448v1 by Dagnachew Mekonnen Marilign, Enrique Molero, Abrham Haile, Louis Powell, Israel Abebe Azime

The real-world usability of ASR models remains challenging, particularly for low-resource languages, where existing speech resources are often collected in clean studio environments. In this work, we introduce TeleASR-Amharic, a benchmark for evaluating Amharic ASR under various acoustic and telecommunication conditions. We analyze the major degradations observed in real telecommunications recordings and develop a benchmark that covers multiple channel conditions, environmental disturbances, and telecom-domain variations. Our results show that telecommunication degradation can substantially reduce recognition performance, making even high-performing ASR models impractical under severe conditions. For example, the WER of the best-performing model increases from 0.23 to 0.79 under severe telecommunication degradation, corresponding to a relative increase of 243% in recognition errors. We further investigate the effects of different noise and channel distortions and show that scaling training data and applying carefully designed telecommunication-aware augmentation substantially improve robustness. In particular, our best training configuration achieves a 23.6% relative WER reduction under extreme noise conditions. Together with the release of our telecommunications-domain data, these findings provide a reproducible benchmark for measuring progress in Amharic ASR while highlighting open challenges in developing robust speech technologies for real-world deployment.

摘要：

##### **Label Authority in Speech Emotion Recognition: Intended and Perceived Ground Truth in Emozionalmente**
2610.26446v1 by Marco Giordano, Stefano Giacomelli, Claudia Rinaldi, Fabio Graziosi

Ground truth in Speech Emotion Recognition (SER) is often presented as a single categorical fact, yet an acted emotion and the emotion heard by listeners describe different aspects of the same recording. We examine this distinction in Emozionalmente, an acted Italian corpus that provides both an elicited production label and five listener judgements for every clip. Using frozen XLS-R, HuBERT, and WavLM representations with matched probes, we compare three label authorities: the expressed condition, the listeners' hard plurality, and their complete vote distribution. The results tell a consistent story. Recovering the expressed condition is easier than predicting a tie-broken listener plurality, but that gap compares two different questions rather than revealing a general loss of SER ability. As listener agreement weakens, the plurality becomes an increasingly brittle hard target. Training on the full vote distribution does not substantially change which class wins the model's hard decision, but it produces probability assignments that more closely follow the alternatives selected by listeners, especially for ambiguous clips. No authority therefore replaces the others. Expressed labels describe how speech was produced, pluralities provide an operational single-class decision, and vote distributions preserve perceptual uncertainty. For human-facing Internet of Sounds applications, evaluations should state which authority is being predicted, retain its aggregation rule and agreement information, and use distributional scores when disagreement is part of the target.

##### **WenSight-Decision-1 for Direct Decisions under E-commerce Rules**
2610.26444v1 by Mingwen Liu, Jun Sun, Sichen Wang

E-commerce decisions require applying natural-language rules to the available case evidence. We introduce WenSight-Decision-1, a shared model that scores supplied options directly from rules and case information for six operational tasks, with examples spanning six languages. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B with reference options as supervision, using the same option scores for training and inference. On 1,440 synthetic operational cases reserved for final evaluation, accuracy rises from 61.60% for Qwen3.5-9B to 92.92% for WenSight-Decision-1 under identical inputs and scoring. Joint fine-tuning improves all six tasks, bringing heterogeneous operational judgments under a common decision interface.

##### **Rethinking LLM Fine-Tuning via Weight Space Reparameterization: Preserving Safety during Downstream Adaptation**
2610.26443v1 by Minseong Kim, Jongbok Won, Jeesup Park, Yonghee Choi, Dongjun Han

Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks often weakens their previously aligned safety behavior, creating an inherent trade-off between task adaptation and safety preservation. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by identifying safety-relevant neurons, layers, or update directions in the original parameter space. However, safety-related information is often distributed across many directions in this space, causing downstream updates to overwrite parameters that encode safety behavior. We propose WSR-Tune, a Weight Space Reparameterization-based fine-tuning framework that preserves safety during downstream adaptation. Our approach constructs a safety-conditioned basis and reparameterizes the weight matrices such that safety-relevant information becomes concentrated in a small subset of directions. We then identify safety-critical directions and freeze them in the reparameterized space, while updating the remaining complementary directions for downstream adaptation. By explicitly structuring the parameter space to disentangle safety and task information, our method reduces interference between objectives and enables simultaneous preservation of safety and improvement in downstream performance. Experimental results show that our method achieves a more favorable trade-off between downstream performance and safety retention, demonstrating its effectiveness for reliable LLM fine-tuning.

摘要：

##### **Fake News, Real Crashes: Information Injection Attacks on LLM Financial Trading Agents**
2610.26438v1 by Mengxiao Wang, Nitesh Saxena

Large language model (LLM) agents now trade real capital using news as evidence, yet they cannot reliably tell fabricated headlines from real news. Because they read the same feeds under near-identical policies, a single fabricated post can push them into the same trade at the same moment. We present a systematic attack benchmark for information injection against LLM trading agents. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. The benchmark spans five injection vectors and seven event categories, evaluated over ~10,000 decisions on 15 U.S. stocks. Five findings emerge. (i) Prompt injection achieves 14% attack success rate (ASR), but fabricated news reaches 62% and two 30-word templates reach 100% on all 15 stocks, inverting the usual LLM-security intuition. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. (iii) Success depends on the agent's state, with a fake indicator panel scoring 91% against an agent about to hold but 12% against one about to buy. (iv) Attacks generalize across model vendors and remain effective when blended, unlabeled, into real news feeds. (v) Most attacks evade standard defenses, since model scaling stops shallow vectors but not fact poisoning, peer debate fails, and only hierarchical aggregation reduces ASR.

##### **The Geometry of Agent Memory: Forecasting Retrieval-Bound Memory Failures, and Characterizing Its Limits**
2610.26433v1 by Iliya Zhechev, Radoslav Ralev, Chris Coleman, Srijith Rajamohan, Jen Agarwal, Tyler Hutcherson, Simba Khadder

We study whether geometric relationships between conversation turns, memory texts, and queries can predict answer correctness before generation. We evaluate four memory implementations with dense or hybrid retrieval and one observation-summary comparator. We embed these texts in a shared space. A logistic classifier uses 25 features describing query proximity, memory-conversation correspondence, and conversation structure. On LongMemEval-small, pooled AUROC is 0.640 over 2,367 question-system pairs, below a question-difficulty baseline of 0.655. Performance varies by question type: geometry reaches 0.841 on single-session-assistant questions, but system identity alone reaches 0.826. After controlling for difficulty and identity, geometry adds no statistically established pooled improvement (delta = +0.002, 95% CI [-0.010, 0.014]). A fixed-store context-selection experiment finds higher accuracy with proximity-ranked memories than with random subsets, consistent with ordinary retrieval relevance. On LoCoMo, nearest-turn similarity has modest predictive value for single-hop questions and near-chance estimates for several other categories. Training uses correctness labels; prediction uses pre-answer features and no additional LLM generation call. The results characterize associations between memory representations and correctness, rather than establish a reliable deployment diagnostic. In particular, embedding proximity does not verify fact coverage or distinguish memory-construction, retrieval, and generation errors.

##### **HarnessRL: Co-Evolving Policies with a Just-in-Time Training Harness for Open-Ended RL**
2610.26426v1 by Xiaoqiang Wang, Ting-En Lin, Yuchuan Wu, Pinyi Zhang, Caixin Kang, Ling Li, Bingxue Qiu, Jieping Ye, Yongbin Li, Bang Liu

Rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) trains policies for open-ended generation with natural-language criteria as rewards, but these rewards can become ineffective as training progresses. We identify two failure modes. Under non-discriminative rewards, scores collapse to uniformly low or high values due to insufficient exploration or a mismatch between task difficulty and policy capability. Under spurious rewards, higher scores do not correspond to better responses. Both reflect a training configuration whose task sampling, exploration, and evaluation stay fixed as the policy evolves. We introduce HarnessRL, a training-time harness that co-evolves with the policy through three interfaces for task sampling, rollout guidance, and rubric criteria. HarnessRL maintains a training-time memory from which a harness evolver retrieves within-task history and cross-task evidence. Using this evidence, the evolver attributes each reward failure to the interface whose update can address it. It then runs a proposer and just-in-time critic loop in which the critic accepts a candidate update only after measuring its effect on the current frozen policy, returning rejected candidates for revision. We also curate Rubric-Mix-10k, a five-domain training and evaluation suite for open-ended RL across medical, science, writing, role-play, and instruction-following tasks. On Rubric-Mix-10k, HarnessRL outperforms the strongest baselines by 2.8 to 3.1 points across both 4B and 9B model scales and nearly halves the training steps to its best checkpoint. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Tongyi-ConvAI/Qwen-Character/tree/main/HarnessRL.

##### **SNO-PI: Label-Scarce SNOMED CT Entity Linking with Pre-trained Encoders and Curated Terminology Knowledge**
2610.26425v1 by Piyawoot Songsiritat

We present SNO-PI, the top-performing submission on SNOMED International's SNOMED CT Entity Linking Benchmark as of 18 October 2026. SNO-PI achieves a macro character IoU of 0.5387, outperforming the organisers' reference system (0.4582; based on the 2024 challenge winner's approach) by 18% relative. Linking clinical mentions to SNOMED CT is a zero- and few-shot problem over a very large label space: the 272 annotated MIMIC-IV discharge summaries cover only 6,595 of the roughly 219,000 eligible concepts, and a third of those occur only once. This motivates a design built on transfer from pre-trained models and human-curated knowledge. SNO-PI combines high-recall span proposal, SapBERT candidate retrieval and listwise ensemble reranking with three BERT-family encoders, each further pre-trained with masked language modelling on unlabelled MIMIC-IV notes and then on SNOMED CT synonyms and hierarchy before task fine-tuning. We find that curated knowledge helps most on concepts never seen in training, yet these concepts remain the main source of error.

##### **Co-Evolving Policy and Reward for Code Generation: A Mechanistic Study of Single-Adapter Drift**
2610.26416v1 by Amarsaikhan Batjargal, Noriko Tomuro

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is particularly well suited to code generation because generated programs can be objectively evaluated through execution. Execution also provides behavioral information that can be reused as additional supervision. The Synergistic Policy and Reward Co-Evolving Framework (SPARK), originally proposed for reasoning tasks, exploits this idea by recycling training rollouts and their evaluation results to train a model not only to generate solutions but also to evaluate and improve them. We investigate whether this policy-reward co-evolution approach transfers effectively to code generation and whether execution-grounded reflection provides an additional source of self-improvement. We compare SPARK-style rollout recycling with execution-only Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) using Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct. Our results do not support either hypothesis under the configuration tested. Although co-evolution improves training performance, these gains do not generalize to held-out problems. Compared at the same stage of training, rollout recycling produces roughly 34x greater KL divergence from the reference policy than execution-only GRPO. Over training, trained tokens per GRPO sequence fall from 185 to 86 as training pass rate increases, while held-out performance declines or fails to sustain early gains. We characterize these patterns as single-adapter policy drift: the shared trainable parameters increasingly depart from pretrained code-generation behavior without corresponding gains in generalization. The results show that higher training reward alone is not evidence of generalizable code self-improvement and highlight the importance of held-out evaluation and drift diagnostics.

##### **Where Does Rhetorical Distortion Live? A Multi-Platform, Multi-Language Measurement of Influencer Content**
2610.26412v1 by Xucheng Yu, Jiangping Chen, Haohan Wang

Much of the manipulation in online influencer content is not false but disproportionate: an accurate fact is framed as historic, urgent, or catastrophic. Fact-checking and clickbait detection are not designed to capture such framing, and little is known about where it originates: with the creator, the topic, the platform, or the audience that replies. We operationalize rhetorical distortion through five literature-grounded dimensions (Significance Inflation, Anxiety Manufacturing, Novelty Claims, Loaded Language, and Temporal Distortion) and measure it with a three-tier pipeline in which bilingual lexical rules propose candidates, negative filters cancel known false-positive contexts, and an LLM verifies or relabels the candidates. Applying the pipeline to 13,060 posts from 330 accounts on six platforms (RSS/newsletters, YouTube, Bluesky, Reddit, Weibo, and X/Twitter), we find that platform is far more strongly associated with account-level distortion than content category (Kruskal-Wallis $\varepsilon^2=0.33$; two-way ANOVA $η_p^2=0.49$ for platform vs. $0.09$ for category). X/Twitter scores highest (DI 7.74 vs. 0.78 for RSS). Classifying tweets and their replies separately shows where this comes from: the reply layer readers see under a post (its top-5 replies) carries 3.5x the distortion of the tweet it answers (7.65 vs. 2.17, $p<10^{-6}$, 36 of 43 accounts), driven by Loaded Language. At equal text length the gap shrinks to 1.5x but remains significant, and creator tweets alone are statistically indistinguishable from YouTube and Bluesky. Two independent human annotators confirm the reply gap, and the platform ordering and the reply gap replicate with GPT-4o as a second classifier. On threaded platforms, measured distortion thus lives largely in the audience layer, which motivates measurement that separates creator and reply text.

##### **Expert L2 English Speech Evaluation on a Gaming PC: Accuracy, Fluency, and Prosody with XLS-R, XEUS, and PhoneticXeus**
2610.26406v1 by Richard Rose

oollim is a locally executable system for evaluating L2 English pronunciation accuracy, fluency, and prosody on a consumer computer. The system combines pretrained XLS-R, XEUS, and PhoneticXeus representations with reference-text features and ridge regressions trained on SpeechOcean762, the Korean Learners' L2 Speech Corpus (English), J-AESOP, and their combinations. On 305 independently held-out recordings assessed by three expert raters, several ridge configurations substantially outperform archived Azure API scores obtained in January 2026 in both Spearman correlation and quadratic weighted kappa across all three constructs. These results support expert speech evaluation on consumer hardware using pretrained representations and construct-specific regressions, without neural-model fine-tuning or calibration on the private evaluation data.

##### **LaMER: Language-Conditioned Multi-Expert Routing for Weakly Supervised Referring Expression Comprehension**
2610.26404v1 by Yu Yu Chang, Tzu-Hsuan Weng, Youssef Mohamed Amin, Mohaned Ahmed Elsayed, Hung-Hsuan Chen, Min-Te Sun, Sherine Shawky

Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) localizes the image region described by a natural language expression, underpinning applications such as autonomous navigation, human-robot interaction, and medical image analysis. Under weak supervision, REC is challenged by the visual-linguistic semantic gap and the difficulty of inferring region-text correspondences from coarse image-level signals alone. Moreover, no single visual encoder captures all complementary properties---semantic alignment, fine-grained texture, and boundary awareness---while naively fusing multiple pretrained encoders yields heterogeneous, hard-to-reconcile representations. We propose LaMER, a weakly supervised REC framework that dynamically aggregates four frozen expert encoders through a language-conditioned Cross-Modal Routing Fusion module, adapting expert selection to each referring expression without instance-level bounding-box supervision. A Cell-Text Matching Head with Negative Anchor Augmentation, trained with a multi-objective loss combining contrastive, reconstruction, alignment, and load-balancing terms, further stabilizes optimization. LaMER achieves 77.67\%, 62.81\%, and 69.49\% Acc@IoU\(\geq 0.5\) on RefCOCO, RefCOCO+, and RefCOCOg, surpassing DViN and WeakMCN by up to 16.49 and 14.49 points, with ablations validating each component.

##### **How Benchmark Graph Construction Shapes Multi-Hop KG Navigation: A Controlled Audit of PathQuestion and PathQuestion-Large**
2610.26399v1 by Eduin E. Hernandez, Sergio A. Diaz, Luis F. Garcia, Nurassyl Askar, Stefano Rini

Multi-hop knowledge-graph question answering (KGQA) benchmarks are often treated as fixed language-and-reasoning tasks, but for navigation-based methods, difficulty also depends on the graph in which those tasks are instantiated. This makes graph construction a consequential but often entangled part of the navigation task. To isolate this effect, we audit PathQuestion (PQ) and PathQuestion-Large (PQL) as graph-navigation benchmarks under a controlled intervention: we hold questions, topic entities, released answers, released path annotations, and family-level splits fixed while replacing each released task-filtered graph with the larger source KG distributed with the benchmark. Source restoration expands the effective navigation search space and graph-consistent answer and path multiplicity while sharply reducing unguided reachability to released answers. We evaluate whether these structural changes persist across navigation policies using structural references, frozen small-language-model navigation policies, and trained MINERVA policies. Across both frozen and trained policies, restoration substantially reduces released-answer accuracy and trajectory fidelity, while graph-consistent evaluation recovers only part of the loss. These results show that benchmark graph construction is part of the navigation task specification rather than merely an implementation detail and can materially shape measured behavior even when the released QA task is held fixed.

##### **Bridged Self-Distillation for On-Policy Learning**
2610.26397v1 by Runsheng Chen, Yihong Liu, Raoyuan Zhao, Michael A. Hedderich, Hinrich Schütze, Matthias Grabmair

On-policy distillation can transfer the reasoning capabilities of a strong teacher to a smaller student. However, distillation from a stronger teacher model faces two mismatches: its solutions may be difficult for a smaller student to imitate, and its token-level probabilities may be incompatible with, or even unavailable to, the student. Existing methods handle these settings through different forms of supervision. To this end, we propose Bridged Self-Distillation (BSD), a unified framework for distilling from any teacher that provides reasoning traces. The model to be distilled from, termed the external teacher, first provides a reasoning trace as privileged context, which can be generated in an offline fashion. The student generates rollouts without this context, while a copy of the student conditioned on it, serving as the self-teacher, scores the same rollouts in the student's own token space. BSD uses the resulting token-level guidance to adjust updates on unsuccessful rollouts, with a verifier providing the outcome signal. As the external teacher is queried only to produce text, the same training procedure applies to all teacher scenarios: shared-tokenizer, cross-tokenizer, and closed API teachers. Across these scenarios, BSD improves over the base model and outperforms baselines tailored to each scenario, offering a novel and unified pathway for distillation. Code is available: https://github.com/TUMLegalTech/Bridged-Self-Distillation

2610.26394v1 by Tianqi Liu, Yandong Guan, Songze Li, Wenkai Wang, Gang Yu, Chengfei Lv

We introduce PACE, a benchmark of 43 enterprise tasks with 750 expert-audited Code Points. It combines requirement reconstruction, criterion construction, and evidence-based evaluation to measure PRD fulfillment and connect delivery gaps to agent behavior. 2610.26383v1 by Yupeng Chang, Yahan Li, Chenlu Guo, Diana Inkpen, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang

##### **PhyPro-FM: A Physics and Prompt-Guided Graph Foundation Model for Contingency Analysis**
2610.26351v1 by Xu Yang, Zhanyuan Sheng, Chenhui Lin, Haotian Liu, Qi Wang, Yue Yang, Wenchuan Wu

The continuous evolution of next-generation power systems, characterized by the high penetration of renewable energy resources and dynamic topology variations, poses formidable challenges to traditional N-1/N-2 contingency analysis in terms of computational efficiency and scalability. While deep learning-based methods offer accelerated inference, existing studies predominantly treat contingency analysis as a pure regression problem, often overlooking the physical essence of grid operation and engineering requirements of real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes a physics and prompt-guided graph foundation model (PhyPro-FM) for generalizable and engineering-viable contingency analysis. The proposed architecture integrates graph neural networks with a learnable global query mechanism and, notably, introduces a prompt-guided learning paradigm. By leveraging historical snapshots as few-shot prompts, the model dynamically captures correlations between target and historical operating conditions, significantly enhancing generalization capabilities under unseen topologies. Furthermore, we design a physics-informed training mechanism that prioritizes branch power flow errors over nodal voltage errors, aligning the optimization objective with critical grid security constraints. To ensure engineering applicability, a physics-informed inference mechanism is also developed, incorporating a residual-based correction module and a specialized post-processing strategy for singular branches to circumvent numerical instability. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that PhyPro-FM outperforms state-of-the-art methods in accuracy and generalization, validating its superior physical consistency and robustness for practical engineering applications.
