##### **What Matters for NVFP4 Training? A Comparative Study of Low-Precision Pretraining Recipes**
2610.24343v1 by Anjulie Agrusa, Andrei Panferov, Elizabeth Wei, Keith Wyss, Paul Gibbons, Erik Schultheis, Jinhang Choi, Frank Sun, Tijmen Blankevoort, Dan Alistarh

Training large language models directly in 4-bit floating-point (FP) formats promises substantial improvements in throughput and energy efficiency. While some recipe design choices have been validated at scale, many promising approaches remain untested beyond small models and short token horizons, leaving open the question of which trends will hold. We present a systematic comparison of recent NVFP4 recipes at medium model scale, evaluating dense 8B and 30B-A3B MoE models trained for up to 1T tokens, with a focus on which ingredients are necessary for accuracy recovery. We propose a final recipe grounded in the principles behind established stable NVFP4 training at scale, incorporating state-of-the-art techniques such as unbiased gradient estimation with lower quantization error than stochastic rounding. To the best of our knowledge, this is the strongest FP4 training result demonstrated at this scale to date in loss gap to BF16. Through ablation studies, we find that: (i) each technique in the optimized recipe measurably improves loss trajectory, (ii) selective high-precision layers are necessary for recovering accuracy at scale, (iii) not all tensors in the backward pass benefit equally from de-biasing, leaving room to apply complementary error-reduction techniques to the remaining tensors.

##### **Continuous Ontology Assurance through External Resources -- An Ontology Engineering Method with a Case Study in Software Language Engineering**
2610.24322v1 by Ralf Lämmel

Domain ontologies require review against knowledge recorded in evolving external resources. We propose COATER, a method that manages executable review components called arrows. Each arrow compares a scoped part of an ontology with a relevant resource and reports support or challenges to its axioms. Challenges may prompt proposals for completion, correction, or restructuring and become tasks for human or agent-assisted triage. Per-axiom coverage summarizes the assessments. We illustrate COATER with the FSL ontology in software language engineering (SLE): arrows review language coverage, language conceptualization, and software-engineering activities. An LLM-assisted linkage exercise increases compliance with a defined Wikipedia-linkage policy from 405 to 600 of 600 entities. A structured catalogue of SLE resources organizes review opportunities.

##### **Learning Administrative Boundary-Aware Location Encoders via Reverse Geocoding**
2610.24306v1 by Lakshay Sharma, Alex Marin

Location encoders learn compact representations of latitude--longitude coordinates for downstream geospatial prediction tasks. Existing approaches are pretrained using continuous or naturally occurring supervision, such as imagery, climate variables, or species distributions, and therefore do not explicitly capture administrative boundaries or place names, despite their strong influence on many real-world geospatial phenomena. We introduce, to our knowledge, the first method for learning administrative boundary-aware location encoders, using reverse-geocoded text as a self-supervised training signal. Our approach can be applied to existing location encoder architectures and consistently improves their downstream performance. We further introduce RevGeoRQ, a custom architecture tailored to reverse-geocoding supervision, which yields additional gains and exceeds contemporary location encoders on several downstream tasks. Finally, the use of human-interpretable reverse-geocoded strings and residual-quantized semantic IDs provides a more interpretable representation of geographic location than existing approaches. Code and model weights to be released soon.

##### **Not Every User Says It All: Training Tool-Using Agents Beyond Ideal Simulated Users**
2610.24294v1 by Han Bao, Zheyuan Zhang, Kaiwen Shi, Yanfang Ye

Tool-using agents are trained and evaluated almost exclusively with strong simulated users who state every detail clearly, yet real users are often weaker. We construct weak users by masking sentences of a simulated user's replies, holding the task and the user's knowledge fixed, and cross-evaluate agents trained with strong and weak users on tau2-bench and ToolWOZ. Weak users lower the success of every policy, and training with them does not help: no policy trained with weak users, through fixed masks, mixtures or a curriculum, serves weak users better than the strong-trained policy beyond run-to-run noise, and prompting the agent to ask more does not help either. We propose StaGPO (State-Grouped Policy Optimization): the agent writes what the task needs, what is missing and how it is being filled, and GRPO uses this state to compare turns of different rollouts that reach the same situation, assigning step-level credit. Within the standard recipe of a small SFT cold start followed by RL, StaGPO makes weak-user training useful: on ToolWOZ, the Qwen3.5-2B policy trained with weak users reaches 76.8% with weak users, the highest of any Qwen3.5-2B policy we trained, 4.8 points above GRPO and 2.8 above the strongest baseline; it is also 5.4 points above GRPO with strong users, and generalises to an unseen mask rate; trained with strong users instead, it serves weak users at least as well as GRPO trained with weak users. On tau2-bench it scores highest with weak users for both models, by a small margin for Qwen3.5-4B.

##### **Language Models are Lazy Poets: Creative Mode Collapse at Scale**
2610.24223v1 by Jacob A. Matthews, Sil Hamilton

While aligned large language models (LLMs) can successfully complete many creative tasks, their outputs tend to be highly self-similar. Though solutions to this problem of "mode collapse" have been proposed, comprehensive large-scale surveys of the phenomenon in specific textual genres are still lacking. We address this gap through an extensive treatment of LLM mode collapse in poetry. Across 15 poetic forms, 248 subject matter tags, and 16 open-weight models, we generate over 1.1 million poems. To our knowledge, this constitutes the largest corpus of LLM-generated poetry to date. At million-poem scale, we find that LLM poetic vocabularies and rhyming abilities are substantial, but severely underutilized. In other words, LLMs are "lazy" poets: despite possessing large poetic inventories, LLMs over-rely on a small number of words, opening phrases, and rhymes. Further, we find that lexical homogenization differs across models and poetic forms, noting observed rhyming vocabulary is correlated with model size.

##### **Self-Improving Video-to-Action Models**
2610.24177v1 by Hyunwoo Ryu, Eric Ming Chen, Max Wilcoxson, Vincent Sitzmann

Video generative models are an emerging tool for embodied agents: they can serve as scalable visual planners, generating videos that depict how to complete previously unseen tasks. However, executing these plans typically requires a video-to-action or "inverse dynamics" model trained on large, human-collected video-action datasets, severely limiting scalability. We introduce Mirror, a self-supervised approach that learns video-to-action mappings through environment interactions, without prior action data or hand-crafted rewards. Mirror learns by attempting to follow a visual plan by executing its best guess of the underlying actions in the environment. Mirror then trains on the resulting video-action pairs, incrementally self-improving and following the visual planner more closely. Across Atari, OGBench, and DeepMind Control, Mirror outperforms leading learning-from-observation methods with learned video plans, and achieves near-expert performance with oracle plans. On Minecraft BASALT, it outperforms methods with access to expert actions or hand-engineered task knowledge. In Minecraft survival, it is the first method to acquire an iron pickaxe through self-improvement alone, without rewards or expert action labels. In the offline setting, with expert action labels but no environment interaction, it sets a new state of the art in obtaining diamonds. Our results suggest that advances in video models can translate into increasingly capable agents without a corresponding need for hand-crafted rewards or large collections of expert action data.

##### **CoDA: A Human-LM Collaborative Codebook Development Assistant for Domain-Specific Text Annotation**
2610.24157v1 by Jaspreet Ranjit, Swaminathan Chellappa, Augusto Rivas Costante, Chandan Manjunath, Laura Onasch-Vera, Hailey Winetrobe Nadel, Eric Rice, Swabha Swayamdipta

We present CoDA, an open-source human-LM collaborative codebook development assistant that helps domain experts develop annotation guidelines for annotating new variables in unstructured text with language models. Existing language model annotation systems primarily focus on downstream classification performance, while assuming the availability of high-quality annotation guidelines, or codebooks. In practice, developing these codebooks is time-consuming and challenging, particularly for concepts that require expert knowledge. CoDA addresses this gap by collaborating with domain experts to iteratively develop codebooks through targeted review of incorrect language model predictions and incorporating expert feedback into the evolving codebook. We demonstrate CoDA on two real-world case studies: extracting health indicators from homelessness case management notes and classifying sentiment of wildlife trafficking posts on social media. Across both domains, CoDA produces codebooks that achieve downstream classification performance comparable to expert codebooks while reducing codebook development time from several weeks to two hours and requiring 5X fewer manually annotated examples. Applying CoDA to generate annotations at scale, we identified 2,410 case notes that contained health discussions or referrals and 10,196 social media posts expressing negative sentiment that may warrant investigation for potential wildlife trafficking. By accelerating codebook development, CoDA helps practitioners use language models to extract domain-specific insights from unstructured text at scale.

##### **lepidoptera: A Domain-Agnostic Engine for Causal Simulation with Mixed Variable Types, Vine Copula Dependence, and Individual-Level Counterfactual Propagation**
2610.24147v1 by Danilo Augusto Sarti

lepidoptera is an open-source R and Python package for simulation from structural causal models with heterogeneous variable types. The package is built around a typed mechanism architecture, which separates the causal graph from the generating rule at each node and exposes each rule as an inspectable, serialisable object. A pre-built mechanism library covers all nine combinations of continuous, categorical, and ordinal parent and child types, removing the need for users to implement type-specific sampling from scratch. A vine copula mechanism estimates the full joint dependence structure from observed data using pair-copula constructions and the Rosenblatt conditional-simulation algorithm, supporting over twenty copula families. Individual-level counterfactual queries are supported through partial propagation, which freezes the random draws of unaffected nodes and replays only the downstream consequences of an intervention, thereby separating the individual treatment effect (ITE) estimand from the population-level average treatment effect (ATE) without additional model assumptions. An extension layer allows any pre-fitted predictive model to serve as a generative mechanism via a unified callable interface. Three worked illustrations spanning clinical trial planning, emergency-ward capacity analysis, and epidemiological mediation analysis demonstrate the package's scope.

##### **Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies**
2610.24137v1 by Guangya Hao, Yiting Duan, Longyan Tan, Yunbo Long, Hao Wu, Ge Zheng, Alexandra Brintrup, Zhuokai Zhao

Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.

##### **GLOBE: Evolving Structured Agentic Memory for E-commerce Product Optimization in Generative Search**
2610.23709v1 by Hyunseo Kim, Suil Choe, Sukkyoung So, Daeyeon Yi

Optimizing product listings for generative search requires language agents not only to rewrite effectively but also to continually self-improve by retaining successful strategies across diverse categories. However, existing approaches often treat structured listings as free-form text, leaving effective strategies implicit in prompts, search trajectories, or rewritten outputs rather than accumulating them as a reusable agentic memory. To address this limitation, we introduce GLOBE, an agentic framework that drives continual self-improvement by storing optimization knowledge in an evolving handbook of explicit, field-scoped behaviors. In each episode, an Editor applies relevant behaviors and receives rank-based feedback, which a Reflector converts into behavior updates. Crucially for dependable execution in real-world settings, a Moderator removes redundant proposals, a Curator commits accepted changes, and consistently harmful behaviors are deactivated. This cycle transforms episodic feedback into a persistent, field-scoped optimization memory. Experiments show that structured behaviors outperform monolithic prompt optimization, while feedback-driven evolution and moderation further improve effectiveness and reduce downside risk. The learned behaviors also remain effective on unseen product categories, suggesting that agentic memory evolution supports interpretable and transferable product optimization.

##### **GEMS: Molecular Structure Elucidation as Learned Search over Isomer Graphs**
2610.23617v1 by Utku Umur Acikalin, Yingheng Wang, Goncalo J. Gouveia, Tyler J. Schwertfeger, Frank C. Schroeder, Carla P. Gomes

Identifying small-molecule structures from molecular formulas and tandem mass spectra remains a bottleneck in untargeted metabolomics and natural-product discovery. Even when database retrieval or de novo prediction misses the exact structure, the resulting candidates can provide starting points for exploring nearby structures through a few bond edits. We introduce GEMS (Guided Editing of Molecular Structures), which refines candidates through learned 2-switch bond edits that preserve the molecular formula and each atom's total valence. GEMS uses MIST, a pretrained spectrum-to-fingerprint model, to predict fingerprint probabilities once per spectrum. A neural policy compares each candidate's fingerprint with these predictions, links present features to the atoms and bonds that generate them, and scores all valid edits in one forward pass. The policy is trained on shortest edit paths between structures, so it can be pretrained on PubChem isomers without spectra and then fine-tuned with spectrum-predicted fingerprints. At inference, GEMS searches from multiple initial candidates matching the supplied molecular formula and ranks candidates by fingerprint agreement and multi-start consensus. With the target structure excluded from PubChem initialization, three edit rounds achieve Hit@1/Hit@10 of 29.64%/50.81% on NPLIB1 and 30.22%/41.33% on MassSpecGym, exceeding published baselines under comparable settings. On NPLIB1, GEMS achieves higher identification accuracy in about 3.3 minutes end to end, compared with 26.2 hours for our FRIGID run. GEMS also improves identification from generated candidates, including those refined by FRIGID. Experiments with exact target fingerprints identify fingerprint prediction error as a bottleneck in target recovery and final ranking. Deeper search recovers additional targets without retraining, with larger gains from weaker initial candidates.

##### **From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents**
2610.23599v1 by Yewei Zhao, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yin Xu, Peilun Zhou, Xiaohan Wang, Guojun Yin, Wei Lin, Mingjun Xiao

Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.

##### **Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents**
2610.23574v1 by Mingze Yin, Xiaohan Wang, Dian Li, Longfei Luo, Jiahong Yan, Yiheng Zhu, Chang-Yu Hsieh

While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.

##### **ConvexLLM: Scaling Convex-Hull Speech Interfaces to General-Purpose Audio Intelligence**
2610.23539v1 by Ming-Hao Hsu, Shujie Liu, Yuxuan Hu, Yuxiang Wang, Yingda Shen, Jinyu Li, Zhizheng Wu

Speech and audio language models connect continuous acoustic representations to decoders pretrained primarily on text data. Most such models use MLP or Q-Former interfaces to map acoustic features into the decoder's input space, requiring the decoder to learn how to interpret the resulting representations. We investigate whether grounding these representations in the decoder's existing text embeddings can help it use acoustic information while better preserving its pretrained text capabilities. We present ConvexLLM, a general-purpose speech and audio language model whose C-Gate interface represents audio as continuous convex combinations of frozen text embeddings. ConvexLLM achieves an eleven-task VoiceBench aggregate of 75.36, the highest among the open-weight models we compare, and an MMAU Test-mini accuracy of 62.50%. In a comparison with a 2B-parameter decoder, C-Gate achieves higher aggregate spoken-language performance than MLP and Q-Former interfaces, with text-task performance close to MLP and task-dependent tradeoffs in transcription and audio understanding. ##### **Truth in Contrast: Calibrated Factual Evidence from Language Model Likelihoods**
2610.23527v1 by Ponhvoan Srey, Jason Lucas, Pittawat Taveekitworachai, Junyoung Lee, Anh Tuan Luu

Large Language Models (LLMs) encode substantial factual knowledge. Yet, estimating confidence in factual claims reliably remains challenging. We study likelihood-based factual confidence from the complementary perspectives of discrimination and calibration across multilingual factual statements. Specifically, we instantiate contrastive generative likelihoods as Contrastive Factual Evidence (CFE), which compares the likelihood of the same claim under competing factual and non-factual conditions, and derive its probabilistic counterpart, the Contrastive Factual Posterior (CFP). We show analytically that CFP calibration depends on the differential error between the two surrogate conditionals, allowing errors shared across them to cancel and yield tighter calibration bounds. Empirically, CFP substantially improves calibration while maintaining factual discrimination comparable to its strongest competitor. Nonetheless, discrimination is uniformly weak across methods in lower-resource languages, which simultaneously undermines the utility of the measured calibration. To this end, we interpret a likelihood geometry, within which an evidence-bearing direction is induced, along with an orthogonal evidence-invariant direction. In our investigation, we selectively align target-language claims with stronger English evidence only along the factual-evidence direction, improving discrimination and open-ended factual generation without target-language labels.

##### **MuninAI: A Self-Hosted Agentic Framework for Independent Research Groups**
2610.23480v1 by Markus Fischer, Jens Meiler, Matthias Elgeti

Research groups that want a language model grounded in their own literature face a choice between hosted services that receive their unpublished work and self-hosted deployments that keep the data local but give the model no access to the corpus. Munin is an open-source framework for a self-hosted scientific assistant that indexes a group's corpus and its citation graph, and wraps whichever open-weight model the group can serve in an agent harness with specialised reading, search, and computation tools. We claim that the harness, rather than the specific model, is what makes the system useful on domain literature, and we ship the evaluation suite that tests this claim with the software.
  On 199 paired literature questions, the harness more than doubles the accuracy of the base model, from 0.387 to 0.874 (+0.487, 95% CI [0.407, 0.568]), while naive retrieve-then-answer scores below the unaided model on every backbone tested. Under the harness, roughly twice as many answer claims trace to a passage the model read itself as with fixed top-5 retrieval. When the source paper for a question is removed from the corpus, the system abstains on about nine in ten of the questions it had answered correctly on the Qwen backbones and eight in ten on gpt-oss-20b, and it produces no fabricated local citation across 300 fabricated references.

With all outbound network access disabled, both Qwen backbones still exceed the published PaperQA2 accuracy. The evaluation suite runs as a single command, so an adopting group can re-run it on its own corpus and model.
